View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` ingredients and `k` grams of magic powder
2. For each ingredient `i`, we need `a_i` grams to bake one cookie
3. We currently have `b_i` grams of ingredient `i`
4. We can use the magic powder to compensate for any missing ingredients
5. We need to find the maximum number of cookies we can bake

## Key Insights

1. For each ingredient `i`, if we want to bake `x` cookies, we need `x * a_i` grams
2. We already have `b_i` grams, so we need `max(0, x * a_i - b_i)` additional grams for ingredient `i`
3. The total additional grams needed across all ingredients is `sum(max(0, x * a_i - b_i))`
4. This total must be ≤ `k` (our magic powder)

## Approach

This is a classic binary search problem. We can binary search on the number of cookies `x` from 0 to some upper bound.

For a given `x`, we can check if it's possible to bake `x` cookies by calculating:
- Total additional grams needed = `sum(max(0, x * a_i - b_i))`
- If this sum ≤ `k`, then `x` cookies is possible

We need to find the maximum such `x`.

## Upper Bound

The maximum possible cookies cannot be more than `(k + total_available_ingredients) / min(a_i)` or something similar. A safe upper bound is when we use all magic powder to compensate for the most deficient ingredient.

Since each cookie requires at least `min(a_i)` grams of any ingredient, and we have `k` grams of magic powder, the maximum cookies can't exceed `(k + sum(b_i)) / min(a_i)`.

But a simpler approach: the worst case is when we have to compensate for all ingredients. The maximum possible cookies would be when we use all `k` grams to compensate for the ingredient with the largest requirement.

Actually, a safe upper bound: since each cookie requires at least 1 gram (minimum a_i is at least 1), and we have at most `sum(b_i) + k` total grams available, the maximum cookies is at most `sum(b_i) + k` (when each ingredient needs exactly 1 gram).

But we need to be more precise. For each ingredient, the maximum number of cookies we can make is `floor((b_i + magic_used_for_i) / a_i)`. 

A safer upper bound: the maximum could be when we use all magic powder to make up for the ingredient that requires the most grams. But actually, we can set an upper bound of `max(b_i) / min(a_i) + k / min(a_i)`.

A simpler approach: upper bound = `max(b_i) / min(a_i) + k / min(a_i) + 1000000` - this might be too large.

Actually, since each cookie requires at least 1 gram of each ingredient, and we have `k` magic powder, the maximum cookies is bounded by `(sum(b_i) + k) / min(a_i)`.

But actually, we can just use a safe upper bound like `10**18` since values can go up to 10^9 and k up to 10^9.

Wait - let's think: in worst case, if one ingredient requires 1 gram per cookie, and we have 10^9 grams of magic powder, and other ingredients have plenty, we could make up to `10^9 + (sum of all b_i)` cookies. Since `sum(b_i)` can be up to `10^5 * 10^9 = 10^14`, the upper bound could be huge.

But note: if an ingredient requires `a_i` grams per cookie, then `x * a_i - b_i ≤ k` for each ingredient (the amount we need to compensate).

So `x ≤ (b_i + k) / a_i` for each ingredient.

Therefore, `x ≤ min( (b_i + k) / a_i )` for all i?

No, that's not right. For each ingredient `i`, we need `x * a_i ≤ b_i + k` (if we use magic powder), so `x ≤ (b_i + k) / a_i`.

But we can't have `x` less than that. Actually, the constraint for ingredient `i` is `x * a_i ≤ b_i + k`, which means `x ≤ (b_i + k) / a_i`.

Therefore, the maximum possible `x` is `min((b_i + k) / a_i)` for all i? 

Wait, no. For a fixed `x`, we need `x * a_i ≤ b_i + k` for each ingredient `i`. This means `x ≤ (b_i + k) / a_i` for each ingredient.

But this gives us a constraint on `x`, so the maximum `x` is the minimum of `(b_i + k) / a_i` over all `i`.

Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: n=1, k=1000000000, a=[1], b=[1000000000]
- x ≤ (1000000000 + 1000000000) / 1 = 2000000000
- Answer is 2000000000, which matches.

Example 2: n=10, k=1, a=[10^9]*10, b=[1]*10
- For each ingredient: x ≤ (1 + 1) / 10^9 = 2 / 10^9 ≈ 2e-9
- So x ≤ 0 (since we can't make fractional cookies)
- Answer is 0, which matches.

Example 3: n=3, k=1, a=[2,1,4], b=[11,3,16]
- Ingredient 1: x ≤ (11+1)/2 = 12/2 = 6
- Ingredient 2: x ≤ (3+1)/1 = 4/1 = 4  
- Ingredient 3: x ≤ (16+1)/4 = 17/4 = 4.25
- So x ≤ 4, which matches the answer.

Example 4: n=4, k=3, a=[4,3,5,6], b=[11,12,14,20]
- Ingredient 1: (11+3)/4 = 14/4 = 3.5
- Ingredient 2: (12+3)/3 = 15/3 = 5
- Ingredient 3: (14+3)/5 = 17/5 = 3.4
- Ingredient 4: (20+3)/6 = 23/6 ≈ 3.83
- So x ≤ min(3.5, 5, 3.4, 3.83) = 3.4, so maximum integer x is 3, which matches.

This is brilliant! So the maximum number of cookies is the floor of the minimum over all ingredients of `(b_i + k) / a_i`.

Therefore, the answer is `floor(min((b_i + k) / a_i))` for i from 1 to n.

## Algorithm

1. Read n, k
2. Read array a (required per cookie)
3. Read array b (available)
4. Find `min((b_i + k) / a_i)` for all i
5. Output the floor of this minimum value

## Implementation

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
b = list(map(int, input().split()))

max_cookies = float('inf')

for i in range(n):
    # For ingredient i, maximum cookies we can make is (b[i] + k) / a[i]
    max_per_ingredient = (b[i] + k) / a[i]
    max_cookies = min(max_cookies, max_per_ingredient)

# Take floor of the result
result = int(max_cookies)
print(result)
```

Wait, let me double-check the logic. The constraint is that for ingredient i, we need `x * a_i ≤ b_i + k`, so `x ≤ (b_i + k) / a_i`. We need to satisfy this for ALL ingredients. The most restrictive constraint gives us the upper bound. So the maximum x is the floor of the minimum of `(b_i + k) / a_i`.

Yes, this is correct.

Let me verify with example 3:
- a = [2,1,4], b = [11,3,16], k = 1
- (11+1)/2 = 6
- (3+1)/1 = 4  
- (16+1)/4 = 17/4 = 4.25
- min = 4, floor = 4 ✓

Example 4:
- a = [4,3,5,6], b = [11,12,14,20], k = 3
- (11+3)/4 = 14/4 = 3.5
- (12+3)/3 = 15/3 = 5
- (14+3)/5 = 17/5 = 3.4
- (20+3)/6 = 23/6 ≈ 3.83
- min = 3.4, floor = 3 ✓

Perfect!

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the maximum number of cookies that can be baked given:
- `n` ingredients
- `k` grams of magic powder
- For each ingredient `i`, we need `a[i]` grams to make one cookie
- For each ingredient `i`, we currently have `b[i]` grams

The magic powder can be used to compensate for any ingredient shortage. We want to maximize the number of cookies we can bake.

## Key Insight

For each ingredient `i`, if we want to bake `x` cookies, we need `x * a[i]` grams of ingredient `i`. We currently have `b[i]` grams, so we need an additional `(x * a[i] - b[i])` grams if `x * a[i] > b[i]`.

The total magic powder needed is the sum of all deficiencies: `∑ max(0, x * a[i] - b[i])`.

We want to find the maximum `x` such that the total magic powder needed ≤ `k`.

## Approach

This is a classic binary search problem on the answer (number of cookies).

- The lower bound is 0 (we can always bake 0 cookies)
- The upper bound can be determined: if we have only k grams of magic powder, and the largest ingredient requirement is `max(a[i])`, then the maximum possible cookies is at most `k / min(a[i]) + max(b[i]) / min(a[i])`, but a safer upper bound is `k + max(b[i])` (since we can at most use k grams of magic powder to compensate).

Actually, let's think more carefully: if we have k grams of magic powder and ingredient i requires `a[i]` grams per cookie, and we have `b[i]` grams, then for `x` cookies, we need `x * a[i] - b[i]` more grams (if positive). The total needed is `∑ max(0, x * a[i] - b[i]) ≤ k`.

The maximum possible `x` could be very large, but note that if `x` is too large, the sum will exceed k. We can set a reasonable upper bound.

Upper bound: since each ingredient requires at least 1 gram per cookie, and we have at most `max(b[i])` grams for each ingredient, the maximum cookies can't exceed `k + max(b[i]) / min(a[i]) + max(b[i]) / max(a[i])`, but a simple safe upper bound is `max(b[i]) + k // min(a[i]) + 1`.

Actually, let's use a simpler upper bound: since we need at least `x * a[i] - b[i]` for each ingredient i where this is positive, and the total sum of these deficits must be ≤ k, if x is very large, the sum grows linearly with x. For a single ingredient with requirement a[i], we need x*a[i] - b[i] grams. Since a[i] ≥ 1, the deficit grows at least linearly with x.

So we can set upper bound as `max(b[i]) + k + 1` might be too conservative.

Better: the maximum possible cookies can't exceed `k + max(b[i]) / min(a[i])` or something. Let me think differently.

Actually, if we consider the component with the smallest requirement, we can't bake more than `(k + max(b[i])) // min(a[i]) + 1` cookies. But this is complicated.

A safe upper bound: since even for the ingredient with minimum requirement, we need x * min(a[i]) - b[i] ≥ 0 for x ≥ b[i]/min(a[i]), and the total magic needed is at least x * min(a[i]) - sum of b[i], but this is not quite right.

Actually, worst case: if all ingredients have requirement 1 (min = 1), and we have b[i] grams for each, then for x cookies, we need x - sum(b[i]) in total, so x ≤ k + sum(b[i]).

But we don't know sum(b[i]). However, since we have k grams of magic powder, the maximum x cannot exceed k + max(b[i]) (in the worst case where all ingredients have requirement 1).

Better upper bound: in the worst case, if we have one ingredient with requirement 1, and we have b[i] grams, then we can bake up to b[i] + k cookies.

Since max(b[i]) ≤ 10^9 and k ≤ 10^9, we can set upper bound as `10**10` to be safe.

Actually, even safer: the maximum x cannot exceed max(b[i]) + k + 1000000000, but let's be practical.

We can set upper bound as `max(b) + k + 1000000000` or simply use `10**10`.

But looking at constraints: n ≤ 100000, k ≤ 10^9, a[i], b[i] ≤ 10^9.

Maximum possible cookies: if we have k grams of magic powder, and the smallest requirement is min_a, then we need additional (x * min_a - sum of b[i] for i where x * min_a > b[i])... 

Actually, since each ingredient contributes at most x * a[i] - b[i] when positive, and the total of these must be ≤ k, if we consider the ingredient with minimum requirement, the deficit grows as x * min_a - some constant. 

So a safe upper bound is k + max(b[i]) // min(a[i]) + max(b[i]) // min(a[i])... 

Actually, the maximum x is at most k + max(b[i]) // min(a[i]) + something.

But to be safe, let's set upper_bound = 2 * 10**10. Since k ≤ 10^9 and max(b[i]) ≤ 10^9, and min(a[i]) ≥ 1, x won't exceed k + max(b[i]) for the ingredient with minimum requirement, but actually even for that ingredient, x can be as large as b[i] + k / min(a[i]) ≤ 10^9 + 10^9 = 2*10^9.

So upper_bound = 2 * 10**9 might be sufficient.

But let's check: if we have one ingredient with requirement 1, and b[i] = 10^9, k = 10^9, then we can bake 10^9 + 10^9 = 2*10^9 cookies. So yes.

Therefore, upper_bound = 2 * 10**9 should be safe.

## Algorithm

1. Read inputs
2. Set lower = 0, upper = 2 * 10**9
3. Binary search on x (number of cookies)
4. For each candidate x, calculate total magic needed = sum(max(0, x * a[i] - b[i]) for all i)
5. If total magic needed ≤ k, then x is feasible, so try larger x
6. Otherwise, x is too large, try smaller x
7. Return the maximum feasible x

## Edge Cases

- If k = 0, we can only bake cookies if b[i] ≥ x * a[i] for all i
- The examples show this works

Let me verify with examples:

Example 1: n=1, k=1000000000, a=[1], b=[1000000000]
For x cookies: need max(0, x*1 - 1000000000)
Total needed: x - 1000000000 ≤ 1000000000
So x ≤ 2000000000. Maximum x is 2000000000. Correct.

Example 2: n=10, k=1, a[i]=1000000000, b[i]=1
For x cookies: need max(0, x*1000000000 - 1) for each ingredient
Total needed: 10 * max(0, x*1000000000 - 1) ≤ 1
If x ≥ 1/1000000000, which means x ≥ 0 (since x is integer ≥ 1), then for x ≥ 1: need 10 * (x*1000000000 - 1) ≥ 10*1000000000 - 10 = 9999999900 > 1
For x = 0: need 0 ≤ 1, so feasible.
Thus maximum x is 0. Correct.

Example 3: n=3, k=1, a=[2,1,4], b=[11,3,16]
Try x=4:
- ingredient 1: max(0, 4*2 - 11) = max(0, 8-11) = 0
- ingredient 2: max(0, 4*1 - 3) = max(0, 4-3) = 1
- ingredient 3: max(0, 4*4 - 16) = max(0, 16-16) = 0
Total needed = 0+1+0 = 1 ≤ 1. Feasible.

Try x=5:
- ingredient 1: max(0, 5*2 - 11) = max(0, 10-11) = 0
- ingredient 2: max(0, 5*1 - 3) = max(0, 5-3) = 2
- ingredient 3: max(0, 5*4 - 16) = max(0, 20-16) = 4
Total needed = 0+2+4 = 6 > 1. Not feasible.

So maximum x is 4. Correct.

Example 4: n=4, k=3, a=[4,3,5,6], b=[11,12,14,20]
Try x=3:
- ingredient 1: max(0, 3*4 - 11) = max(0, 12-11) = 1
- ingredient 2: max(0, 3*3 - 12) = max(0, 9-12) = 0
- ingredient 3: max(0, 3*5 - 14) = max(0, 15-14) = 1
- ingredient 4: max(0, 3*6 - 20) = max(0, 18-20) = 0
Total needed = 1+0+1+0 = 2 ≤ 3. Feasible.

Try x=4:
- ingredient 1: max(0, 4*4 - 11) = max(0, 16-11) = 5
- ingredient 2: max(0, 4*3 - 12) = max(0, 12-12) = 0
- ingredient 3: max(0, 4*5 - 14) = max(0, 20-14) = 6
- ingredient 4: max(0, 4*6 - 20) = max(0, 24-20) = 4
Total needed = 5+0+6+4 = 15 > 3. Not feasible.

So maximum x is 3. Correct.

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    n, k = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    b = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Binary search on the number of cookies
    left, right = 0, 2 * 10**9
    
    while left < right:
        mid = (left + right + 1) // 2
        
        # Calculate total magic powder needed for mid cookies
        total_needed = 0
        for i in range(n):
            needed = mid * a[i] - b[i]
            if needed > 0:
                total_needed += needed
        
        if total_needed <= k:
            left = mid
        else:
            right = mid - 1
    
    print(left)

solve()
```

The binary search uses `(left + right + 1) // 2` to avoid infinite loops when left == right, and we're searching for maximum feasible value.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` ingredients and `k` grams of magic powder
2. For each ingredient `i`, we need `a[i]` grams to make one cookie
3. We currently have `b[i]` grams of ingredient `i`
4. We can use the magic powder to supplement any missing ingredients
5. We need to find the maximum number of cookies we can bake

## Key Insight

For each ingredient `i`, if we want to make `x` cookies, we need:
- `x * a[i]` grams of ingredient `i`
- We already have `b[i]` grams, so we're short by `max(0, x * a[i] - b[i])` grams

The total magic powder needed is the sum of shortages across all ingredients:
`total_shortage = sum(max(0, x * a[i] - b[i]) for all i)`

We have `k` grams of magic powder, so we need:
`sum(max(0, x * a[i] - b[i]) for all i) ≤ k`

We want to maximize `x`.

## Solution Approach

This is a classic binary search problem. We can binary search on the number of cookies `x` from 0 to some upper bound.

**Upper bound consideration:**
- The maximum possible cookies is when we use all magic powder to compensate for the most deficient ingredients
- In the worst case, if one ingredient requires very large amounts, we might need many cookies
- Since each ingredient requires at least 1 gram per cookie, and we have at most `k` grams of magic powder, we can't make more than `(total deficit) + (some limit)` cookies
- A safe upper bound: the maximum possible cookies is when we consider that even with magic powder, we still need to satisfy the minimum requirements
- We can use `max(0, sum(b[i] / a[i]) for all i)` as a base, plus some safety margin, but actually a simpler approach is to use a very large number like `10**18`

**Algorithm:**
1. Binary search on the number of cookies `x` from 0 to a large upper bound (like `10**18`)
2. For each candidate `x`, calculate the total magic powder needed
3. If total needed ≤ k, then `x` is achievable, so we try a larger value
4. Otherwise, we need fewer cookies

## Step-by-step Implementation

```python
def solve():
    n, k = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    b = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Binary search on the number of cookies
    left, right = 0, 10**18
    
    while left < right:
        mid = (left + right + 1) // 2
        
        # Calculate total magic powder needed to make 'mid' cookies
        total_needed = 0
        for i in range(n):
            # For ingredient i, we need mid * a[i] grams
            # We have b[i] grams, so shortage is max(0, mid * a[i] - b[i])
            shortage = max(0, mid * a[i] - b[i])
            total_needed += shortage
        
        if total_needed <= k:
            left = mid
        else:
            right = mid - 1
    
    print(left)

solve()
```

## Verification with Examples

**Example 1:** n=1, k=1000000000, a=[1], b=[1000000000]
- To make x cookies: need x * 1 = x grams, have 1000000000, so shortage = max(0, x - 1000000000)
- Total needed = max(0, x - 1000000000)
- We need max(0, x - 1000000000) ≤ 1000000000
- So x - 1000000000 ≤ 1000000000 → x ≤ 2000000000
- Maximum x = 2000000000 ✓

**Example 2:** n=10, k=1, all a[i]=1000000000, all b[i]=1
- To make x cookies: need x * 1000000000 for each ingredient
- We have only 1 gram per ingredient, so shortage per ingredient = max(0, x * 1000000000 - 1)
- Total shortage = 10 * max(0, x * 1000000000 - 1)
- This needs to be ≤ 1
- So 10 * (x * 1000000000 - 1) ≤ 1 → x * 1000000000 ≤ 1.1 → x ≤ 0.0000000011
- So maximum x = 0 ✓

**Example 3:** n=3, k=1, a=[2,1,4], b=[11,3,16]
- Try x=4: 
  - Ingredient 0: need 4*2=8, have 11 → shortage = 0
  - Ingredient 1: need 4*1=4, have 3 → shortage = 1  
  - Ingredient 2: need 4*4=16, have 16 → shortage = 0
  - Total shortage = 1 ≤ 1 ✓
- Try x=5:
  - Ingredient 1: need 5*1=5, have 3 → shortage = 2
  - Total shortage = 2 > 1 ✗
- So maximum x = 4 ✓

The solution works correctly.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` ingredients and `k` grams of magic powder
2. For each ingredient `i`, we need `a_i` grams to bake one cookie
3. We currently have `b_i` grams of ingredient `i`
4. We want to find the maximum number of cookies we can bake using our existing ingredients plus the magic powder

## Key Insights

1. To bake `x` cookies, we need `x * a_i` grams of ingredient `i`
2. We currently have `b_i` grams of ingredient `i`, so we need `max(0, x * a_i - b_i)` more grams of ingredient `i`
3. The total magic powder needed is the sum of all these deficits: `sum(max(0, x * a_i - b_i))`
4. We want to find the maximum `x` such that this total deficit is ≤ `k`

## Approach

This is a classic binary search problem:
- The answer (number of cookies) can range from 0 to some upper bound
- For a given number of cookies `x`, we can calculate how much magic powder is needed
- We can use binary search to find the maximum `x` where the required magic powder ≤ `k`

## Step-by-step Solution

1. **Determine search bounds**:
   - Lower bound: 0 cookies
   - Upper bound: We need to find a reasonable upper limit
   - For each ingredient, if we have `b_i` and we need `a_i` per cookie, the maximum cookies we could make with just that ingredient is `b_i // a_i`
   - Since we have magic powder, the upper bound can be set to `(total_deficit_with_0_cookies)` but we need a more conservative estimate
   - A safe upper bound: if we consider the worst case, we could potentially make `(max(b_i) // min(a_i) + k // min(a_i))` cookies, but let's use a simpler bound

2. **Function to check if `x` cookies are possible**:
   - For each ingredient `i`, calculate deficit = `max(0, x * a_i - b_i)`
   - Sum all deficits
   - If sum ≤ k, then `x` cookies is possible

3. **Binary search**:
   - Search for the maximum `x` where the required magic powder ≤ k

Let me trace through the examples:

**Example 1**: n=1, k=1000000000, a=[1], b=[1000000000]
- To make x cookies, we need x * 1 = x grams
- We have 1000000000 grams, so we need max(0, x - 1000000000) grams of magic powder
- We want max x such that x - 1000000000 ≤ 1000000000
- So x ≤ 2000000000
- Answer: 2000000000 ✓

**Example 2**: n=10, k=1, a=[10^9]*10, b=[1]*10
- To make x cookies, we need x * 10^9 grams for each ingredient
- We have 1 gram per ingredient, so we need max(0, x*10^9 - 1) per ingredient
- Total needed = 10 * max(0, x*10^9 - 1)
- For x ≥ 1, this is ≥ 10 * (10^9 - 1) which is way > 1
- So x must be 0 ✓

**Example 3**: n=3, k=1, a=[2,1,4], b=[11,3,16]
- For x cookies, we need:
  - Ingredient 1: max(0, 2x - 11)
  - Ingredient 2: max(0, 1x - 3)
  - Ingredient 3: max(0, 4x - 16)
- Total needed: (2x-11) + (x-3) + (4x-16) = 7x - 30 (for x ≥ 6)
- For x=4: 7*4 - 30 = 28 - 30 = -2, so the max terms are 0 for all (since 2*4=8<11, 4<3? no, 4>3, so 4-3=1, 16>16? no)
- Actually: 2*4=8 ≤ 11, so 0
- 1*4=4 ≥ 3, so 1
- 4*4=16 ≤ 16, so 0
- Total: 1 ≤ 1, so x=4 is possible
- For x=5: 2*5=10 ≤ 11, 1*5=5 ≥ 3, 4*5=20 ≥ 16, so need (5-3) + (20-16) = 2 + 4 = 6 > 1
- So maximum is 4 ✓

## Implementation

```python
def can_bake(x, a, b, k):
    """Check if we can bake x cookies with available ingredients and magic powder"""
    total_magic_needed = 0
    for i in range(len(a)):
        required_for_x = x * a[i]
        if required_for_x > b[i]:
            total_magic_needed += required_for_x - b[i]
        # If we have enough, no additional magic powder needed
    return total_magic_needed <= k

def solve():
    n, k = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    b = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Binary search on the number of cookies
    left, right = 0, 10**18  # Upper bound is large enough
    
    while left < right:
        mid = (left + right + 1) // 2
        if can_bake(mid, a, b, k):
            left = mid
        else:
            right = mid - 1
    
    print(left)

solve()
```

Wait, let me reconsider the upper bound. The maximum possible cookies would be when we use all magic powder optimally.

Since each ingredient i needs a[i] grams per cookie, and we have b[i] grams, the theoretical maximum for ingredient i is b[i] // a[i]. But with magic powder, we can potentially make more.

The worst case: one ingredient has very large a[i] but small b[i], so we need many cookies. 

A safe upper bound would be something like max(b[i] // a[i] + k // min(a[i]) + 1000000) but let's be simpler.

Actually, consider: if we have magic powder k, and each cookie needs at least min(a) grams of the worst ingredient, the maximum cookies can't exceed (sum of all b[i] + k) // min(a), but this is complex.

A safer bound: in the worst case, if we have one ingredient with a[i] = 1 and b[i] = 1, then we can make up to (1 + k) cookies. Since k ≤ 10^9, and we have n ≤ 100000, a safe upper bound is 10**18, which is more than sufficient.

Yes, 10**18 is safe because even if we need to cover all ingredients with magic powder, the total needed grows linearly with the number of cookies, and our baking constraint would stop the search when it exceeds k.

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
b = list(map(int, input().split()))

def can_bake(x):
    total_magic_needed = 0
    for i in range(n):
        required = x * a[i]
        if required > b[i]:
            total_magic_needed += required - b[i]
        # If we have enough, no additional magic powder needed
    return total_magic_needed <= k

# Binary search for maximum number of cookies
left, right = 0, 2 * 10**18

while left < right:
    mid = (left + right + 1) // 2
    if can_bake(mid):
        left = mid
    else:
        right = mid - 1

print(left)
```

n, k = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
b = list(map(int, input().split()))

def can_bake(x):
    total_magic_needed = 0
    for i in range(n):
        required = x * a[i]
        if required > b[i]:
            total_magic_needed += required - b[i]
        # If we have enough, no additional magic powder needed
    return total_magic_needed <= k

# Binary search for maximum number of cookies
left, right = 0, 2 * 10**18

while left < right:
    mid = (left + right + 1) // 2
    if can_bake(mid):
        left = mid
    else:
        right = mid - 1

print(left)

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

The term of this problem is the same as the previous one, the only exception — increased restrictions.


-----Input-----

The first line contains two positive integers n and k (1 ≤ n ≤ 100 000, 1 ≤ k ≤ 10^9) — the number of ingredients and the number of grams of the magic powder.

The second line contains the sequence a_1, a_2, ..., a_{n} (1 ≤ a_{i} ≤ 10^9), where the i-th number is equal to the number of grams of the i-th ingredient, needed to bake one cookie.

The third line contains the sequence b_1, b_2, ..., b_{n} (1 ≤ b_{i} ≤ 10^9), where the i-th number is equal to the number of grams of the i-th ingredient, which Apollinaria has.


-----Output-----

Print the maximum number of cookies, which Apollinaria will be able to bake using the ingredients that she has and the magic powder.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` ingredients and `k` grams of magic powder
2. For each ingredient `i`, we need `a[i]` grams to make one cookie
3. We currently have `b[i]` grams of ingredient `i`
4. We can use the magic powder to compensate for missing ingredients
5. We want to find the maximum number of cookies we can bake

## Key Insight

For each ingredient `i`, if we want to make `x` cookies, we need `x * a[i]` grams of ingredient `i`. 

Since we already have `b[i]` grams, we need `max(0, x * a[i] - b[i])` additional grams for ingredient `i`.

The total additional grams needed across all ingredients should not exceed our magic powder `k`.

So: `sum(max(0, x * a[i] - b[i]) for all i) ≤ k`

We want to maximize `x`.

## Solution Approach

This is a classic binary search problem on the answer. We can binary search on the number of cookies `x` from 0 to some upper bound.

**Upper bound**: In the worst case, we might need to make many cookies. The maximum possible cookies would be when we use all magic powder to compensate for the deficit. Since each cookie needs at least 1 gram of some ingredient, and we have at most `k` grams of magic powder, the maximum cookies can't exceed `k + max(b[i])` in practice. But more conservatively, if all ingredients have very high requirements, we might need to consider that we can only make cookies if the total deficit can be covered.

Actually, a safe upper bound would be: `max(b[i]) / min(a[i]) + k` or something similar. But simpler: since each cookie requires at least 1 gram, and we have at most `k` grams of powder, the maximum cookies is at most `k + max(b[i]) / min(a[i])`... 

Actually, let's think differently. The maximum number of cookies we can make is limited by how much we can compensate. The total deficit across all ingredients for `x` cookies is:
`sum(max(0, x * a[i] - b[i]))`

We want this sum ≤ k.

The worst case for the deficit is when we have very high requirements and low supplies. But the upper bound can be set as: since we can't make more cookies than the total "potential" we have, and each ingredient can support at most `b[i]/a[i]` cookies (if we ignore powder), plus we can compensate with powder.

Actually, a safe upper bound: if we have `x` cookies, the deficit is `sum(max(0, x * a[i] - b[i]))`. This function is increasing with `x`, so we can binary search on `x`.

A reasonable upper bound: the maximum possible cookies would be when we use all magic powder optimally. Since even for the ingredient with the smallest requirement, we can make at most `b[i]/a[i] + k/a[i]` cookies. But since we have to satisfy all ingredients, we can set the upper bound to `max(b[i]/a[i]) + k` or something.

Actually, a safe upper bound is `max(b[i]) // min(a[i]) + k // min(a[i]) + 1000` but this might be too complex.

A simpler approach: the maximum number of cookies cannot exceed `k / min(a[i]) + max(b[i]) / min(a[i])` and since values can be up to 10^9, we need a safe bound.

Let's set the upper bound to `max(b[i]) // min(a[i]) + k // min(a[i]) + 1000000` - but this might not be safe.

Actually, in the worst case, if we have one ingredient that requires 1 gram per cookie and we have only 1 gram of it, but k is 10^9, then we can make up to 10^9 + 1 cookies.

So the upper bound should be at least `k + max(b[i]) / min(a[i])`.

Since `min(a[i]) ≥ 1`, the maximum cookies we can make is at most `k + max(b[i])`.

But `max(b[i])` can be up to 10^9, and k up to 10^9, so maximum cookies could be up to 2*10^9.

Let's set upper bound to `2000000000` (2 * 10^9) which is safe.

## Algorithm

1. Read input
2. Binary search on the number of cookies from 0 to 2 * 10^9
3. For each candidate number of cookies `mid`, calculate the total deficit: `sum(max(0, mid * a[i] - b[i]))`
4. If total deficit ≤ k, then `mid` is achievable, try larger values
5. Otherwise, try smaller values
6. Return the maximum achievable cookies

## Step-by-step with examples

Example 1: n=1, k=1000000000, a=[1], b=[1000000000]
- For x cookies, we need x * 1 = x grams
- We have 1000000000 grams, so deficit = max(0, x - 1000000000)
- We need x - 1000000000 ≤ 1000000000
- So x ≤ 2000000000
- Maximum x = 2000000000 ✓

Example 2: n=10, k=1, a=[1000000000]*10, b=[1]*10
- For x cookies, we need x * 1000000000 grams for each ingredient
- We have 1 gram each, so deficit for each ingredient is max(0, x * 1000000000 - 1)
- Total deficit = 10 * max(0, x * 1000000000 - 1)
- This is ≥ 10 only when x ≥ 1/1000000000, so for x ≥ 1, deficit ≥ 10 > 1
- So maximum x = 0 ✓

Example 3: n=3, k=1, a=[2,1,4], b=[11,3,16]
- For x cookies: need 2x, x, 4x grams respectively
- We have 11,3,16 grams
- Deficit: max(0,2x-11) + max(0,x-3) + max(0,4x-16)
- Try x=4: max(0,8-11)+max(0,4-3)+max(0,16-16) = 0+1+0 = 1 ≤ 1 ✓
- Try x=5: max(0,10-11)+max(0,5-3)+max(0,20-16) = 0+2+4 = 6 > 1 ✗
- So answer is 4 ✓

Example 4: n=4, k=3, a=[4,3,5,6], b=[11,12,14,20]
- Try x=3: need 12,9,15,18
- Have 11,12,14,20
- Deficit: max(0,12-11)=1, max(0,9-12)=0, max(0,15-14)=1, max(0,18-20)=0
- Total deficit = 1+0+1+0 = 2 ≤ 3 ✓
- Try x=4: need 16,12,20,24
- Have 11,12,14,20
- Deficit: 16-11=5, 12-12=0, 20-14=6, 24-20=4
- Total deficit = 5+0+6+4 = 15 > 3 ✗
- So answer is 3 ✓

```python
def solve():
    n, k = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    b = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Binary search on the number of cookies
    left, right = 0, 2000000000
    
    while left < right:
        mid = (left + right + 1) // 2
        
        # Calculate total deficit for mid cookies
        total_deficit = 0
        for i in range(n):
            needed = mid * a[i]
            if needed > b[i]:
                total_deficit += needed - b[i]
        
        if total_deficit <= k:
            left = mid
        else:
            right = mid - 1
    
    print(left)

solve()
```

def solve():
    n, k = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    b = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Binary search on the number of cookies
    left, right = 0, 2000000000
    
    while left < right:
        mid = (left + right + 1) // 2
        
        # Calculate total deficit for mid cookies
        total_deficit = 0
        for i in range(n):
            needed = mid * a[i]
            if needed > b[i]:
                total_deficit += needed - b[i]
        
        if total_deficit <= k:
            left = mid
        else:
            right = mid - 1
    
    print(left)

solve()
